The law punished violence, cruelty, and malicious neglect of children. Incidents of child abuse were reported. The Federal Ministry for Family, Seniors, Women, and Youth sponsored programs throughout the year on the prevention of child abuse. The ministry continued to create networks among parents, youth services, schools, pediatricians, and courts, and to support existing programs at the state and local level. Other programs provided therapy and support for young survivors of sexual abuse.\nChild,\nEarly\n,\nand\nForced Marriage\n:\nThe legal minimum age for marriage was 18 years.\nThe law nullified existing marriages conducted in other countries in which at least one spouse was younger than age 16 at the time of the wedding, even if they were of legal age in the country where the marriage was performed. Individuals ages 16 or 17 could petition a judge on a case-by-case basis to recognize their foreign marriage if they faced a specific hardship from not having their marriage legally recognized. Complete central statistics were unavailable on such cases. Child and forced marriage primarily affected girls of foreign nationality.\nSexual Exploitation of Children\n:\nThe law prohibited commercial sexual exploitation, sale, offering, or using children for commercial sexual exploitation, including child sex trafficking, as well as practices related to child pornography. Authorities enforced the law. The minimum age for consensual sex was 14 years, but sex was not considered consensual if the person was younger than age 16 and the older partner was age 18 or older and “exploiting a coercive situation.” It was illegal for adults to offer compensation for sex to a person younger than age 18. It was also illegal for a person age 21 or older to have sex with someone between ages 14 and 16 if the older person “exploits the victim’s lack of capacity for sexual self-determination.”\nThe law allowed undercover investigators to use artificially created videos of child sexual abuse to gain access to internet forums. The government’s independent commissioner for child sex abuse issues operated an online help portal and an anonymous telephone helpline free of charge.\nIn August, North Rhine-Westphalia (NRW) Minister of Interior Herbert Reul said the number of child pornography cases in NRW rose to 11,000 annually in 2021 and 2022 from 1,578 in 2013, with a successful prosecution rate of about 80 percent. Reul attributed the higher number of reported cases to better investigation and enforcement methods, and the state’s efforts to combat the problem more decisively.\n\nANTISEMITISM\nObservers estimated the country’s Jewish population to be approximately 200,000, of whom an estimated 90 percent were from the former Soviet Union. According to the Central Welfare Board of Jews in Germany, there were approximately 91,900 registered, dues-paying members of Jewish congregations at the end of 2022, the most recent figures available.\nManifestations of antisemitism, including physical and verbal attacks, occurred at public demonstrations, sporting and social events, in schools, in the street, in certain media outlets, and online. Apart from antisemitic speech, desecration of cemeteries and Holocaust monuments represented the most widespread antisemitic acts, although there was a rising number of physical assaults on individuals perceived to be Jewish.\nThe federal government attributed most antisemitic acts to neo-Nazi or other right-wing extremist groups or persons, and such acts increased during the year. According to a preliminary figure obtained by the socialist Left party caucus from the Ministry of Interior, 1,225 crimes were motivated by antisemitism in the first three quarters of the year; in the third quarter the number of crimes increased 76 percent over the same period in 2022.\nIn March 2022, the Ministry of Interior published a 10-point plan to combat right-wing extremism, and in November 2022, the federal government released the country’s first national strategy to combat antisemitism and support Jewish life. Jewish organizations also noted antisemitic attitudes and behavior of some Muslim youth and left-wing extremists. NGOs agreed right-wing extremists were responsible for most antisemitic acts but cautioned federal statistics misattributed many acts committed by Muslims as right-wing acts.\nThe number of antisemitic incidents rose sharply in the fourth quarter of the year concurrent with conflict in the Middle East. The NGO RIAS, which collects and monitors reports of antisemitism, announced on October 18 it recorded 202 antisemitic incidents from October 7 to 16, up 240 percent over the same period in 2022. This included tweets, chants, vandalism, and graffiti, as well as physical attacks on buildings and persons. In Berlin, unknown persons threw Molotov cocktails at a synagogue complex on October 18, but there were no injuries or damage. RIAS reported antisemitic demonstrations in Berlin, North Rhine-Westphalia, Bavaria, Baden-Wuerttemberg, and Lower Saxony in October.\nAccording to the Deutsche Presse Agentur, in the early morning of July 17, five volunteer firefighters of the Leonberg, Baden-Wuerttemberg, fire department shouted antisemitic and right-wing extremist slogans through the fire engine’s loudspeakers while driving through the city. Police investigated charges of inciting racial hatred. The firefighters were suspended by the city.\nIn its report released February 6, the panel of scholars reviewing accusations of antisemitism at the 2022 Documenta Fifteen art exhibition and the handling of them concluded that Documenta Fifteen became “an echo chamber for Israel-related antisemitism, and sometimes for pure antisemitism.” In its report, the panel accused the curators and certain artists who exhibited at the Documenta of a clear antisemitic bias, including through works that promoted stereotypes.\nIn April, a court in Cologne ruled three of the four men charged with assaulting and seriously injuring in 2021 a Jewish man, age 18, who said he was wearing a kippa in a public park would have to pay the defendant €400-500 each ($432-541) for the pain they inflicted upon him. The victim, however, could not remember whether the assailants had used antisemitic insults, and the judge ruled it was not possible to determine whether antisemitism was a motive in the attack. The fourth suspect was no longer in the country.\nMany prominent government officials repeatedly condemned antisemitism throughout the year, including Federal Chancellor Olaf Scholz, Federal President Frank-Walter Steinmeier, Federal Minister of Economics and Climate Robert Habeck, Federal Interior Minister Nancy Faeser, and Federal Minister of Education Bettina Stark-Watzinger. In 2018, the federal government created the position of federal commissioner for Jewish life in Germany and the fight against antisemitism. Since then, 15 of 16 states also established state-level commissioners to combat antisemitism. In the one state that had not instituted the position, the local Bremen Jewish community advised the state government such a position was not necessary. The community deemed alternative tools to combat antisemitism to be more efficient. The positions’ responsibilities varied by state but involved meeting with Jewish communities, collecting statistics on antisemitic acts, and designing education and prevention programs. A federal- and state-level Commission to Combat Antisemitism and Protect Jewish Life that included all commissioners met twice a year to coordinate strategies.\nTRAFFICKING IN PERSONS\nSee the Department of State’s\nTrafficking in Persons Report\nat\nhttps://www.state.gov/trafficking-in-persons-report/\n.\nACTS OF VIOLENCE, CRIMINALIZATION, AND OTHER ABUSES BASED ON SEXUAL ORIENTATION, GENDER IDENTITY OR EXPRESSION, OR SEX CHARACTERISTICS\nCriminalization\n:\nNo laws criminalized consensual same-sex sexual conduct between adults, cross-dressing, or other sexual or gender characteristic-related behaviors.\nViolence\na\nnd\nHarassment\n:", "excerpt_sha256": "45d919107572fdef8c5ad949961b77ea7de198a3979f696d7cde84359b9146c7", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R01-E008", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; The CJEU decided that the age at the time of the original\n     application for international protection is decisive in both scenarios. However, this ruling only spoke\n     of family reunification of refugees. In December 2022, the Federal Administrative Court ruled that a\n     distinction between refugees and beneficiaries of subsidiary protection concerning the right to family\n     reunification does not violate the Constitution.28 The CJEU has again strengthened its ruling\n     concerning the time of the child’s minority, but its application to subsidiary protection beneficiaries by\n     German Courts remains open. In October 2023, the Federal Administrative Court ruled that\n     international protection status due to family reunification can be revoked, if the ‘principal’ person\n     entitled to international protection dies29 (see Family reunification).\n\n\nTemporary protection\n\nThe information given hereafter constitute a short summary of the German Report on Temporary\nProtection, for further information, see Annex on Temporary Protection.\n\n     v Key statistics on temporary protection: as of February 2024, 1,139,689 persons were\n       registered in the Central Register of Foreigners, and 941,559 persons held a residence permit\n       for temporary protection. As of January 2024, 65% of registered persons are women, and 30.7%\n       are under 18 years old.\n\nTemporary protection procedure\n\n     v Scope of temporary protection and disadvantageous treatment of third country nationals:\n       In Germany the scope of temporary protection is wider than in the Council decision. Temporary\n       protection is awarded to Ukrainian nationals and their family members, which includes spouses,\n       non-married partners, minor children and other close relatives if there is a ‘dependency\n       relationship’ that was already established prior to entering Germany. In 2023 however, two\n       administrative Courts expressed contradicting positions concerning non-married partners.\n       Whereas a Court in Cologne decided that a permanent partnership can be established according\n       to its exclusivity and willingness to support each other financially and emotionally, a Court in\n       Munich did not consider permanent partnership as equivalent to married couples.\n       Temporary protection may also be granted to third country nationals holding a residence permit\n       in Ukraine, even if not a permanent one, but only if they are unable to return to their countries of\n       origin. It has been criticised that in practice applicants from third countries face several\n       disadvantages in all stages of the procedure compared to Ukrainian nationals. The different\n       treatment between third country nationals applying for temporary protection and applicants of\n       Ukrainian nationality constitutes the most flagrant legal disputes as to the German\n       implementation of the Council Decision. The difference in treatment is displayed in the registration\n       process, where access to the application for temporary protection is sometimes not granted to\n       third country nationals or where eligible third country nationals are pressured into the asylum\n       procedure instead. After having lodged their application, third country nationals are further treated\n       differently with regards to access to the labour market and other social benefits. There have been\n       legal practitioners and Courts condemning this practice. However, a differentiation still takes\n       place.\n\n     v No automatic suspensive effect upon appeal: Differing from the asylum procedure, the appeal\n       of a negative decision on temporary protection does not have automatic suspensive effect, an\n       application for interim measures must be filed to guarantee legal stay during the appeal\n       proceedings.\n\n28\n       Federal Administrative Court, Voraussetzungen für den Familiennachzug zu subsidiär Schutzberechtigten,\n       press release Nr. 78/2022, 8 December 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3YcL6rO.\n29\n       Federal Administrative Court, Decision BVerwG 1 C 35.22, 11 October 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/4bHhFFT.\n\n\n                                                      20\n\nv Accessibility of information on temporary protection: The German government is putting\n     effort into distributing information for people fleeing Ukraine on entry, legal stay and housing on\n     their websites in different languages.\n\n   v Flaws in identification of vulnerable groups: As for the asylum procedure no systemic\n     mechanisms exist to identify applicants with special needs and meet these needs.\n\n   v Access to regular social benefits: As of June 2022, applicants and beneficiaries of temporary\n     protection have access to regular social benefits. The unequal treatment between applicants for\n     temporary protection who receive regular social benefits and applicants for international\n     protection who receive social benefits under the Asylum Seekers Benefits Act has been criticised\n     by many civil society organisations, including in 2023.\n\nContent of temporary protection\n\n   v Excessive waiting periods for receiving residence permits: Depending on the region,\n     decision making may take several months due to a general overburdening of the local authorities.\n\n   v Obligation to reside in the allocated municipality also applies to beneficiaries of temporary\n     protection. Following legal amendments in June 2022 beneficiaries of temporary protection are\n     also obliged to reside in the municipality to which they have been allocated in the determination\n     procedure for three years. However, North Rhine-Westphalia suspended the application of this\n     obligation for beneficiaries of temporary protection.\n\n   v Dense housing situation and reintroduction of emergency shelters: While most of the\n     emergency shelters of 2016 had been dismantled, the rising numbers of people fleeing Ukraine\n     but also the continuing high numbers of people fleeing Iran, Syria and Afghanistan in combination\n     with the general lack of affordable housing led the authorities to reintroduce emergency shelters.\n     The conditions in the emergency shelters but also in overcrowded first accommodation centres\n     have been criticised by many NGOs. While beneficiaries of temporary protection are generally\n     allowed to access the regular housing market, due to the lack of affordable housing, they are\n     often required to stay in accommodation centres for extended periods. Thus, beneficiaries of\n     temporary protection are still often living in critical housing conditions, including in 2023.\n\n   v Access to regular social benefits, labour market and health care: Beneficiaries of temporary\n     protection in theory have the same access to social benefits and health care as German\n     nationals. Practical hurdles may however arise due to administrative requirements.\n\n\n\n\n                                                  21", "excerpt_sha256": "e382bce5c2bfa82f41da47e9c0acbb735c74caeb6a99b2e390ccdfa2b0b545d9", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R01-E013", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "Germany introduced temporary stationary border controls with Poland, the Czech Republic, and Switzerland on 16 October 2023 and extended them in December; NGOs argued that the controls caused refusals of entry and denied some people access to BAMF assessment, while a police union said they shifted routes rather than reducing irregular immigration.", "question_ids": ["3B.5", "3B.8", "4B.9", "8B.6", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The controls were introduced and extended during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The controls were introduced by the Federal Government; the source does not establish Scholz’s personal authorship or decision process.", "limitations": ["The source reports stakeholder assessments and does not establish the legality or causal effect of the controls conclusively."], "start_unit": 28, "end_unit": 28, "locator": "page 28", "exact_excerpt": "refusal of entry (Zurückweisung); out of these, 4,489 persons were removed to Austria.60 The general\nnumber is higher than for the first half of 2022 (8,986) and probably due to increased border controls.\n\nGermany has regularly re-introduced border controls at its borders with Austria since 2015. On 16 October\n2023, controls were also introduced at the border with Poland, the Czech Republic and Switzerland and\nwere extended again in December 2023 and March 2024.61 The agreement with Switzerland also includes\ncontrols on Swiss territory. This allows the authorities to prevent people from entering German territory\nand they are then consequently not responsible for any asylum requests.62 The prolongations occurred\ndespite a ruling of the CJEU of 26 April 2022 in which the court states that border controls cannot exceed\na duration of 6 months unless there is a new threat justifying a renewed introduction of controls for another\nsix months maximum.63 The extension has been continually criticised by NGOs such as PRO ASYL, who\nargue that controls lead to refusals of entry of would-be asylum seekers in Germany, who are denied\naccess to an assessment by the BAMF of whether Germany might be responsible for handling their\nasylum application.64 A representative of the union of police officers repeatedly criticised the extensions,\non the grounds that they do not reduce irregular immigration but rather shift routes to other land borders.65\n\nIn 2018, following a heated political debate, a new procedure was introduced which enables the Federal\nPolice to refuse entry at the border and send persons back to Greece and Spain within 48 hours if they\nhave previously applied for asylum there.66 This procedure is based on administrative regulations and\nspecial administrative readmission agreements with the two countries. These returns are therefore not\nbased on the Dublin Regulation, but on a refusal of entry under the (national) notion of ‘safe third countries’\nin combination with administrative arrangements concluded with other EU Member States. Since 2019, it\nwas only applied to persons found at the Austrian-German border, as this was the only border where\ncontrols continue to take place. While being heavily debated in 2018, the introduction of the new\nprocedure had little effect in practice: between August 2018 and May 2021, only 50 persons were returned\n(46 returns to Greece and 4 to Spain) on the basis of the readmission agreements with these countries.67\nWhile no refusals of entry were carried out between May 2021 and the end of 2021 according to the\nFederal Police,68 two persons were returned to Spain each year in 2022 and 2023 and none to Greece.69\nTherefore, the political debate over the return procedures at the border, which had even triggered a\ngovernment crisis in 2018, has been described as ‘absurd’ in retrospect.70\n\n\n\n\n60\n      Ibid, 8.\n61\n      Dw.de, Innenministerin Faeser verlängert Grenzüberwachung, 8 December 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/48iodYA; European Commission, ‘Temporary Reintroduction of Border Control’, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3HLwSGd.\n62\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/8274, 07 September 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/49GtKZX, 21.\n63\n      CJEU. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "Germany revised BAMF vulnerability-identification guidance in 2022 and used internal 2023 guidelines, but the Asylum Act still lacked clear duties to provide adequate support; NGOs and a federal working group continued to report that identification was not systematic, and the federal government did not systematically monitor implementation by the Länder.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "6B.3", "6B.5", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The institutional finding and 2023 criticism apply during the target year, although some reforms predate 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The federal government and BAMF established the guidance and monitoring framework; reception implementation was substantially assigned to the Länder.", "limitations": ["The source reports NGO and working-group assessments rather than a nationwide outcome evaluation.", "The report does not isolate decisions personally made by Scholz."], "start_unit": 92, "end_unit": 94, "locator": "page 92 through page 94", "exact_excerpt": "In June 2022, the BAMF published a revised version of the concept523 as well as standardised forms with\nwhich the Federal States can communicate detected vulnerabilities and specifics to the BAMF and vice\nversa.524 According to the BAMF, the procedures to identity vulnerabilities are laid down in its internal\nregulations, while the concept gives the BAMF staff comprehensive information on the detection and\ntreatment of vulnerable persons and is binding in so far as the internal guidelines refer to it.525\n\nAccording to the BAMF, the identification of vulnerable applicants as required by the APD is primarily the\nremit of the Federal States, who are responsible for reception and accommodation. Additionally, since\n2022 the BAMF internal guidelines lay down the standards for the BAMF employees to identify\nvulnerabilities in order to guarantee a fair asylum procedure for the persons concerned.526 These\nguidelines are updated regularly and are to be used conjointly with the concept mentioned above. A 2016\namendment to the German Asylum Act introduced wording relevant to the identification of vulnerable\nasylum seekers by allowing Federal States to transmit personal information about an applicant’s\nvulnerabilities to the BAMF. In turn, the BAMF has the obligation to transmit relevant information on\nvulnerabilities to the Federal States if they are necessary for adequate accommodation.527\nHowever, lacking clear duties of identification, the Asylum Act still fails to properly transpose the recast\nAPD, as it only requires the BAMF to ‘duly carry out’ the interview and not to provide ‘adequate support’\nto applicants in need of special procedural guarantees throughout the duration of the procedure.528 In\npractice, therefore, identification procedures in Germany have been described as ‘a matter of luck and\ncoincidence’, given that authorities ‘are not able to systematically undertake the necessary steps to\nascertain mental disorders or trauma.’529\n\nPrior to the revision of the law on counselling in 2023, the BAMF stated that the counselling service for\nasylum-seekers, consisting of general information on the procedure as well as the opportunity to make\nindividual appointments with BAMF staff (see Information for asylum seekers and access to NGOs and\nUNHCR) has led to vulnerabilities ‘being partially identified more often’ as counsellors inform applicants\nabout rights of vulnerable applicants during the procedure. As of 1 January 2023, the BAMF provides\nadditional funding for independent counsellors providing support for vulnerable groups.530 The\nindependent counsellors are required to transmit personal information to the BAMF and the Federal States\nagencies responsible for the accommodation of asylum seekers which is relevant for the identification\nand support of vulnerable groups, if the applicant consents.531 However, no details were given concerning\nthe number or the type of vulnerabilities which were identified in the course of the new advice service.\nAccording to information provided by the BAMF, no data are collected on vulnerabilities detected during\nthe counselling nor on the number of vulnerable persons applying for asylum in Germany.532 The BAMF\naffirms that the funding for independent counsellors is dependent on relevant certifications and\n\n\n\n523\n      BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. In 2023, the Federal working group on psycho-social\ncentres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs acknowledged that there\nhave been attempts mainly by the Federal States to address these shortcomings. However, the working\ngroup repeated the criticism of no systematic approach.536 Along with these policy demands, the\nassociations introduced a toolbox on vulnerabilities which provides guidance for counselling and the\nidentification of vulnerabilities.537\n\nThe procedures and practice of identification in reception centres, which are run by the Federal States,\nvary. Upon initial registration, all asylum seekers should undergo a medical examination, which usually\ntakes place shortly after the registration of the asylum application in the arrival centre. However, this\nexamination is focused on the detection of communicable diseases and does not include a screening for\npotential vulnerabilities. Sometimes medical personnel or other staff members working in the reception\ncentres inform the BAMF if they recognise symptoms of trauma, the BAMF provides notification sheets\nwith which vulnerabilities can be communicated but there is no systematic procedure in place ensuring\nthat such information is passed on.538\n\nAs of 2020, only three Federal States (Berlin, Brandenburg and Lower Saxony) had a structured\nprocedure in place to identify particularly vulnerable asylum seekers.539 A number of States conduct\nscreenings, offer psychiatric or psychological consultations or refer to the general care infrastructure, and\nsome Federal States have integrated identification in their concepts for protection from violence in\nreception centres (Hamburg, Hesse, North Rhine Westphalia, Rhineland-Palatinate, Saarland,\nSaxony, Schleswig-Holstein).540 Since 2021, a project led by the Federal working group on psycho-\nsocial centres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs as well as the BAMF\nand local authorities has developed a concept to identify vulnerable applicants in reception centres and\nin psycho-social centres. The concept was piloted in two reception centres in North Rhine Westphalia and\nBremen and the findings were published in March 2023 in a toolbox of guidelines which provide guidance\nfor counselling and the identification of vulnerabilities.541 The working group continues its work, and the\nFederal government affirms that it supports those Federal States who are interested in implementing the\n\n\n\n\n533\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC.\n534\n      KOK, Betroffene von Menschenhandel im Asylkontext erkennen. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin. If the questionnaire indicates a potential vulnerability,\na screening interview takes place with the socio-psychological service of the Brandenburg immigration\nauthority (Zentrale Ausländerbehörde). Following the screening interview, if a vulnerability is detected\napplicants are referred to psychiatric counselling (which only takes place in Eisenhüttenstadt) and can be\nhoused in a special house for vulnerable applicants. The vulnerability is also communicated to the BAMF.\nHowever, in 2016 this was the case for under 1% of all asylum seekers, indicating that detection rates are\nvery low compared to the estimated prevalence of psychological distress among asylum seekers.\nFurthermore, the special accommodation houses both single men with psychological difficulties and single\nwomen who might have been victims of sexual violence.553 The Brandenburg Refugee Council criticises\nthat while there is a coordinated approach to identify vulnerabilities, the support measures vary depending\non the local governance. Accordingly, in some regions no specialised services for the accommodation of\nasylum seekers with special needs are available.554\n\nIn Lower Saxony, projects to improve the identification of vulnerable groups have been established in\nreception centres first in Friedland in 2012 and have since then expanded to all reception centres in the\nFederal State.555 Upon registration, all applicants are informed about special vulnerabilities during a\nmeeting with the social service of the reception centre and a further diagnosis is carried out in cases\nwhere there are indications of psychological disorders. While the authorities usually follow the\nrecommendations which follow from the diagnosis, between 2015 and 2017 only very few people were\nreferred for a diagnosis.556\n\nIn e.g., Rhineland-Palatinate,557 the regional government has adopted a protection concept which also\nincludes methods for the identification of vulnerabilities.558 This includes the following measures:\n     v Obligation to check for possible vulnerabilities in the reception centres during the initial stages of\n         the reception process and the asylum procedure;\n     v Intensification of communication between various actors and authorities involved in the reception\n         system and in the first steps of the asylum procedure;\n     v Documentation of possible vulnerabilities in a data system used by all authorities involved in the\n         reception process and in the asylum procedure;\n     v Training measures for persons employed by the Federal State in the reception centres to raise\n         awareness on the different forms of vulnerabilities.\n\n551\n      Berlin Senate, Senat beschließt Verfahren zur Unterstützung für besonders schutzbedürftige Geflüchtete aus\n      der Ukraine, 5 April 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48NlJCt.\n552\n      Refugee Council Berlin, Kein Ort für Schutzsuchende: Notunterkunft im Flughafen Tegel schließen, 13 March\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Ufa7Um.\n553\n      See BafF, ‘Identifizierung besonderer Schutzbedürftigkeit am Beispiel von Personen mit\n      Traumafolgestörungen. For\n1,608 of those this measure was ended due to an age assessment.575\n\n      2. Special procedural guarantees\n\n                                    Indicators: Special Procedural Guarantees\n\n      1. Are there special procedural arrangements/guarantees for vulnerable people?\n                                                           Yes        For certain categories      No\n             v If for certain categories, specify which: Unaccompanied children, traumatised persons\n                                                         Victims of torture or violence\n\n         2.1. Adequate support during the interview\n\nThe BAMF does not have specialised units dealing with vulnerable groups. According to the BAMF, all\ncase workers complete the EUAA training module on ‘Interviewing Vulnerable Persons’.576 If an applicant\nor a Federal State authority submits information to the BAMF that indicates vulnerability (such as medical\nrecords or information about specific physical, mental, intellectual or sensory impairments), such\ninformation is transferred to the case worker in charge who can decide to take measures such as allocate\nmore time for the interview, appoint an interpreter of a specific gender or allow the person to bring a\ntrusted person of their choice to the interview.577\n\nFor specific groups of vulnerable persons, the BAMF employs ‘special officers’ (Sonderbeauftragte)\nresponsible for interviews and decisions on claims by applicants with special needs. Special officers also\nadvise their colleagues in dealing with vulnerable applicants and are contact persons for specialised\ncounselling services and psycho-social centres.578 Staff members who become special officers must\ncomplete a training module for the specialisation they want to achieve. In addition, they follow the EASO\ntraining modules for their specialisation.579 Training covers both the identification and in the treatment of\nvulnerable persons.580 According to the BAMF, continuous training is offered for specific topics in the realm\nof the special officers’ responsibilities.581 The BAMF guidelines stipulate that the following cases shall be\nhandled in a particularly sensitive manner and, if necessary, by specially-trained decision-makers:582\n     v Unaccompanied children;\n     v Victims of gender-specific prosecution;\n     v Victims of human trafficking; and\n     v Victims of torture and traumatised asylum seekers.\n\nAs of June 2023, a total of 1,267 BAMF had one or more roles as special officers. This corresponds to\nroughly a third of full-time equivalent positions allocated to the first instance procedure (see Number of\n\n574\n        Nerea González Méndez de Vigo, Alterseinschätzung – ein Irrgarten ohne Ausweg? The distribution among areas of responsibilities was the\nfollowing: Unaccompanied children (410), victims of gender-specific persecution (312) traumatised\npersons and victims of torture (291), victims of trafficking (254).583\n\nSpecially trained case officers may be included at all times of the asylum procedure, or take over, also\nprior to the interview, if vulnerabilitites are known. For example, if it becomes evident during the interview\nthat an asylum seeker belongs to one of these groups, the officer conducting the interview is obliged to\nconsult a special officer, in addition to notifying the reception centre if necessary and authorised by the\napplicant.584 A note on how the officers are planning to proceed must be added by the special officer to\nthe file, particularly if the special officer takes over the case as a result of their consultation. According to\ninformation provided by the government, there is an obligation in cases of unaccompanied minors for\nspecial officers to take over responsibility for the asylum procedures. In other cases of other vulnerable\ngroups, the specially trained case officer must be consulted and there are two options for further\nprocedures: either the special officer adopts an advisory role or they take over responsibility for the\nprocedure.585 However, the BAMF does not record the number of cases in which special officers are\nconsulted or in which procedures are handed over to special officers.586\n\nLawyers have reported that the introduction of special officers has led to some improvement in the\nhandling of ‘sensitive’ cases. The special officers receive special training in the area of LGBTIQ, they\nconduct interviews upon consultation by other BAMF officers and serve as multipliers for their\ncolleagues.587However, there have also been examples of cases in which indications of trauma and even\nexplicit references to torture did not lead to special officers being consulted.588 It has further been criticised\nthat there are shorcomings in the effective implementation of procedural guarantees for LGBTIQ+\npersons, which increases the risk of false decisions.589 There is no individual right to have a special officer\nhandling a person’s case, except for unaccompanied minors for whom this is mandatory. But if evidence\nsuggests that the person is vulnerable or if the person claims to have certain vulnerabilities, the interviewer\nis required to involve a special officer in the procedure, e.g. as consultant.590 However, the Administrative\nCourt of Berlin ruled that if special vulnerabilities have been detected, the absence of a special officer in\nthe asylum procedure constitutes a violation of procedural rights of vulnerable asylum seekers.591\n\n        2.2. Exemption from special procedures\n\nThe German Asylum Act exempts neither unaccompanied children nor persons with special procedural\nguarantees from the airport procedure, despite an express obligation under the recast APD to provide for\nsuch exemptions under certain conditions.592 It also makes no reference to ‘adequate support’ which\nshould be provided to those requiring special procedural guarantees.593\n\n\n\n\n583\n       The government notes that the figures cannot be added since some officers may have qualified in more than\n       one area; Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/7503, 28 June 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sb12cF, 2-4.\n584\n       BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Since there are no federal rules governing the access, it is up to the discretion of the local\nauthorities whether welfare associations have access to the centres. In Munich, the Refugee Council\ntried to provide independent mobile counselling prior 2023 and has been denied access. The Federal\nAdministrative Court upheld the denial in 2023. The court decided that access must be granted in\nindividual case after registration and only where an asylum applicant has demanded counselling.\nHowever, local authorities are not obliged to grant open access to the facilities.718 This leads to legal\nuncertainty as to whether systematic access will be provided to welfare associations under the new rules\non counselling. Overall, several associations criticise that due to the lack of funding, the uncoordinated\nfunding process and the legal uncertainty as to whether access to accommodations centres is provided,\naccess to individual counselling cannot be guaranteed in Germany.\n\nPrior to the reforms in January 2023, government advice covered the period from the lodging of the asylum\napplication to the explanation of a first instance decision; now the legal conselling can also cover appeal\nproceedings.719 According to the BAMF, the staff who offered the counselling underwent a one-week\ntraining and was ‘organisationally separated from the asylum area’.720 Procedure counselling was first\nintroduced in a pilot project together with welfare associations.721 It was then established first in all AnkER\nand functionally equivalent centres and has been rolled out to the rest of the BAMF branch offices since\n2020.722\n\nAs of 31 December 2022, counselling was available in 46 BAMF branch offices.723 Throughout 2022,\n37,644 applicants took part in the first stage counselling, while 3,147 received individual counselling\n(second stage). This is an increase in comparison to 2021 (1,928 individual sessions, while 25,784\npersons took part in group sessions), but still shows that only around 15% of the 244,132 persons who\napplied for asylum in 2022 (see Statistics) received individual advice. More information on counselling\nduring the Covid-19 outbreak can be found in the 2021 Update to the AIDA Country Report for Germany.724\nNo information on the availability of counselling and on the number of sessions is available for 2023 as of\nApril 2024.\n\nThe BAMF counselling sessions represent an improvement compared to the situation prior to August 2019\nwhen no information was systematically provided to asylum seekers.725 Nevertheless, the system is\nheavily criticised by NGOs as group counselling sessions tend to be organised within a very short period\nbefore the personal interview with the BAMF and the information provided is limited (i.e. the BAMF tends\nto provide general information on the asylum procedure, sometimes focusing only on asylum seekers’\nobligations and also on information which has nothing to do with the procedure, such as the so-called\n‘return options’).726\n\nIn addition to the counselling services as regulated by the asylum act, asylum seekers are orally informed\nabout ‘the significance and the proceedings of the interview’ and they are instructed about their rights and\n\n\n718\n      Federal Administrative Court, Decision 1 C 40.21, 28 March 2023, available in Germant at:\n      https://bit.ly/480lN0o, para. 27f.\n719\n      Section 12a (2) German Asylum Act.\n720\n      See AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2022, April 2023, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UChWUr, 99.\n721\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023. Asylum seekers reportedly undergo the airport procedure without understanding the\napplicable rules and steps729 (see also Border procedure (border and transit zones)). The welfare\nassociation Caritas hopes that the funding for independent counselling will also enhance the availability\nof counselling services at the airport but asserts for 2023 that there is not enough available data yet to\nevaluate whether there have been any improvements.730\n\n      2. Access to NGOs and UNHCR\n\n                              Indicators: Access to NGOs and UNHCR\n      1. Do asylum seekers located at the border have effective access to NGOs and UNHCR if they wish\n         so in practice?           Yes              With difficulty         No\n\n      2. Do asylum seekers in detention centres have effective access to NGOs and UNHCR if they wish\n         so in practice?           Yes             With difficulty         No\n\n      3. Do asylum seekers accommodated in remote locations on the territory (excluding borders) have\n         effective access to NGOs and UNHCR if they wish so in practice?\n                                         Yes              With difficulty          No\n\nWelfare organisations and other NGOs offer free advice services which include basic legal advice.731\nHowever, access to NGOs is highly dependent on the place of residence. In some reception centres,\nwelfare organisations or refugee councils have regular office hours or are located close to the centres so\nasylum seekers can easily access the offices of such organisations. However, offices of NGOs do not\nexist in all relevant locations and in any case, access to such services is not systematically ensured. As\nof 2023, there is no mechanism at the federal level which ensures that asylum seekers are getting access\nto legal advice from an independent institution before the interview. In contrast, the Federal Administrative\nCourt decided in 2023 that there is no obligation to provide regular access to reception centres for welfare\nassociations. Only in cases where counselling was explicitly requested by the asylum applicant and the\nrespective welfare association received a mandate to counsel this individual applicant, access needs to\nbe granted.732 It is uncertain how the legal reforms of 2023 to the independent counselling will affect the\nimplementation of the court ruling (see: Provision of information on the procedure).\n\nIn AnkER centres in Bavaria, access of NGOs depends on the management of the centre. In the AnKER\nRegensburg for example, Caritas, Amnesty International, the Refugee law clinic Regensburg and\nCampus Asyl have access to the facility, while in Manching/Ingolstadt, only Caritas has established\npresence. In the experience of certain NGOs, asylum seekers are not systematically re-directed to NGOs\nfor further information. In centres such as Manching/Ingolstadt and Regensburg, NGOs have further\nno way of ensuring systematic counselling sessions with every new arrival, since they do not receive the\nregistration list of residents in the AnKER centre.733\n\n727\n        Section 24 (1) Asylum Act.\n728\n        BAMF, DA-Asyl (Dienstanweisung Asylverfahren) – Belehrungen (internal directives of the BAMF), version as\n        of 1 January 2023, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3J5jPTA.\n729\n        ECRE, Airport procedures in Germany Gaps in quality and compliance with guarantees, April 2019, available\n        at: https://bit.ly/2QgOmAH.\n730\n        Caritas, Auch im Schnellverfahren am Flughafen die Rechte wahren, 11 December 2023, available in German\n        at: https://bit.ly/49eEcHY.\n731\n        A database of advice services for asylum seekers is available at: https://bit.ly/2Ho73Az.\n732\n        Federal Administrative Court, Decision 1 C 40.21, 28 March 2023, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/480lN0o, para. 27f.\n733\n        ECRE, The AnkER centres Implications for asylum procedures, reception and return, April 2019, available at:\n        https://bit.ly/2W7dICZ.\n\n\n                                                        117\n\nIn other arrival or AnkER centres established since 2016, access to NGOs is made even more difficult as\nthese do not have offices in the town or region where the new centres are located. A positive example is\nthe arrival centre at Heidelberg where the Federal State of Baden-Württemberg has established an\nindependent ‘qualified social and procedural advisory service’ in cooperation with welfare\norganisations.734 Within this model, a social worker from an independent organisation functions as contact\nperson for 100 asylum seekers and is explicitly commissioned to offer advice on the asylum procedure\n(while in many other reception centres social workers are not necessarily independent and/or they often\nare neither qualified nor entitled to offer counselling services on the asylum procedure).735 Even here, in\nthe past it has proven difficult for the social workers to effectively prepare asylum seekers for the interview\nin the asylum procedure since they are often approached with other urgent matters such as social support,\nfamily reunification etc.736\n\nFurthermore, despite an attempt at a progressive approach in Heidelberg interviews have been scheduled\nat very short notice in the arrival centres, at a time when asylum seekers have to come to terms with other\nadministrative regulations and with their new surroundings in general. In this situation, it has proven\ndifficult to create an adequate setting for the preparation for the interview.737 In the light of these problems\nbeing described in the context of the ‘arrival centre’ at Heidelberg, it can be concluded that access to\nNGOs is even more limited or may be excluded in many other locations where no similar structures exist.\nThis is particularly the case for the possibilities to access NGOs before the interview, since fast-tracking\nof procedures is taking place at a growing number of ‘arrival centres’ and AnkER-centres.\n\nFollowing an initial period in a reception centre, asylum seekers are usually referred to accommodation\ncentres or apartments in other places of residence (see Types of accommodation). Some of these\naccommodation centres are located in remote areas without proper access by means of public transport.\nIf the place of residence is located far away from the next town, travel costs to get there may also pose a\nserious problem in practice, since these costs would only be covered by public funds in exceptional cases.\nAccordingly, access to NGOs can be severely restricted under such circumstances.\n\nThe so-called ‘geographical restriction’ or ‘residence obligation’ (Residenzpflicht) also poses a legal\nobstacle for many asylum seekers who wanted to contact an NGO or lawyer. Beyond the obligation to\nstay in initial reception centres, a general residence obligation is imposed for asylum seekers from safe\ncountries of origin for the whole duration of their procedures (see Freedom of movement).738 Therefore\nthe ‘residence obligation’ and the obligation to remain in a particular reception centre pose serious\nobstacles for access to NGOs and UNHCR in many cases.\n\nFor information on access to NGOs during the airport procedure, see Border procedure (border and transit\nzones).\n\n\n\n\n734\n      Friedrich-Ebert-Stiftung, Das Ankunftszentrum Heidelberg als ‚Pate‘ für Ankerzentren?, 8 August 2018,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/3HKoSqV.\n735\n      Ibid.\n736\n      Ibid.\n737\n      Johannes Moll, ‘Das verkürzte Asylverfahren im Ankunftszentrum Heidelberg. Reference source not\nfound.Subsequent applications). The number of ‘upgrade appeal’ cases and decisions remains high,\nhowever, likely as a result of long court procedures. Between January and the end of May 2023, courts\ndecided on 5.736 such appeals, and in 806 cases (14%) granted asylum or refugee protection, while in\n4,930 cases (86%) the appeal did not lead to an improvement in the protection status.753 9,525 such\nappeals of Syrian nationals were pending as of 31 May 2023, a similarly high number to the end of 2022\n(9,458).754\n\nThe removal ban for Syria that had been in place since 2012 expired at the end of December 2020. The\nban was based on a common decision of the Federal States and the Federal government, but could not\nbe renewed due to disagreement regarding the possibility to remove criminals and ‘persons posing a risk’\nrelated to terrorist activities (‘Gefährder’).This was heavily criticised by NGOs and organisations such as\nthe German Institute for Human Rights, UNHCR and Caritas.755 The removal statistics for the first half of\n2023 indicate that 410 removals of Syrian nationals took place.756 However, Syria is not listed as a country\nof destination for removals in the first half of 2023, meaning that the removals of Syrian nationals took\nplace to other countries, for example to other EU Member States in the form of Dublin transfers or\nremovals following a refusal of entry.757 As of February 2023, the Federal Government declared that it\ncurrently sees no possibilities for removals to Syria.758\n\n      2. Afghanistan\n\nEmergency evacuation since the Taliban takeover in 2021\n\nWith the takeover of the Taliban on 15 August 2021, the German government started an evacuation\noperation for German nationals in Afghanistan as well as Afghan nationals who had worked for German\nauthorities, the military and ‘especially endangered persons’. Between 16 and 26 August 2021, a total of\n5,300 persons were evacuated, out of which 4,400 Afghan nationals. The evacuated persons entered\nGermany via an emergency visa (based on Section 14 and 22 Residence Act).759 Upon arrival, the BAMF\nthen examined whether persons had already been granted permission for an admission from abroad\n(Section 22 Residence Act). If this was not the case, and if the Federal Ministry decided no such\npermission could be granted, persons were informed of this and of the possibility to apply for asylum in\nGermany. 760\n\nAfter the end of the evacuation, German authorities continued to receive and examine notifications of risk\nof former employees and of “especially endangered individuals” in exceptional circumstances. Sub-\ncontractors and consultants who worked for German authorities only indirectly are considered on a case\nby case basis according to the Federal Government. 761 If the examination confirms that the persons are\nat risk due to their work for a German authority, admission permissions continue to be granted according\n\n753\n       Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The and Community.762 Permissions are also included for close family\nmembers (spouses and minor siblings), other relatives are only considered in hardship cases.763 Since\ntravelling out of Afghanistan is difficult and costly, the Federal Government has provided 32 million Euros\nin funding to the GiZ (the German Development Agency) to support persons in leaving the country. It has\nbeen criticised that despite the acknowledgement that fleeing Afghanistan is difficult, there is no\nsystematic evacuation scheme.764 As a result, there have been reports that persons who managed to\ndepart from Afghanistan have been pushed back by Bulgarian, Turkish and Greek border police.765\nPersons admitted to Germany mainly leave via Pakistan and Iran. 766 Germany has also concluded an\nagreement with the government of Pakistan to allow the concerned persons to enter Pakistan with a\nTazkira K (ID card) instead of a passport as required by the Taliban to leave the country. However, in mid-\nSeptember 2022 the Federal Government reported that a total of 34 former employees and family\nmembers are known to have died in Afghanistan.767\n\nAdmission schemes\n\nGermany has been operating an admission scheme for local staff of German ministries in Afghanistan\nsince 2013. The scheme is based on Art. 22 (2) Residence Act (Temporary residence permission to uphold\nthe political interest of the Federal Republic of Germany). The eligibility criteria depend on the status of\nthe former employee. Only former staff (and their close family members) directly employed by German\nentities are covered by the programme.768\n\nFrom the takeover of the Taliban in 2021 to 17 October 2022, according to the Federal Government,\n38,100 persons had been issued a permission for admission to Germany (out of which 24,500 were former\nemployees and eligible family members, and 13,600 were especially vulnerable persons and their eligible\nfamily members). Around 26,000 of these (68.2%) persons had entered Germany up until that time. As of\n10 December 2021, a total of 28,053 permissions for admission from abroad had been issued to Afghan\nnationals and 8,014 persons had entered Germany as of the same date.769 The admission scheme for\nlocal staff continues in parallel to the new humanitarian admission scheme announced on 17 October\n2022.\n\nOn 17 October 2022, the Federal Government launched an additional federal admissions programme\nwhich had been announced in the coalition agreement of 2021.770 The government describes the\nprogramme and procedure as follows: the programme is geared towards persons who ‘have exposed\nthemselves to particular risk through their commitment to women’s and human rights or their work in the\nspheres of justice, politics, the media, education, culture, sport or academia and are thus vulnerable’ or\n‘due to the special circumstances of their individual cases have experienced or are experiencing violence\nor persecution based on their gender, sexual orientation or gender identity or religion and are therefore at\nconcrete and personal risk. In particular, these are victims of serious individual women’s rights violations,\nhomo- or transphobic human rights violations or vulnerable representatives of religious\n\n\n\n\n762\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community, Reply to written question by Canan Bayram (The Greens),\n      20/5046, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3NvUljF, 81-121.\n763\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 11.\n764\n      Taz.de, Die Mauern werden höher, 27 February 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48d59Ln.\n765\n      Ibid.\n766\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 2.\n767\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 16.\n768\n      ECRE, Afghans Seeking Protection in Europe, December 2021, available at: https://bit.ly/3krGfED, 11.\n769\n      Deutscher Bundestag, parliamentary question by The Left, 20/791, 22 February 2022, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3RPrRUC, 1.\n770\n      AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2021, April 2022, available at: https://bit.ly/3XnN7RS, 11.\n\n\n                                                      122\n\ngroups/communities.’771 The admission programme includes family members of those persons, which\nincludes spouses or same sex partners, minor children and other family members who can prove a\nrelation of dependency (beyond economic dependency) with the main person and find themselves in a\nsituation of concrete and lasting danger due to the work or vulnerability of the main person.772 The German\ngovernment appoints agencies (including civil society organisations) who can put forward names of\nsuitable persons, who must still be living in Afghanistan, via an IT application containing a questionnaire\nof a total of 41 pages.773 The names of these organisations are not made public by the government, but\naccording to a press report, PRO ASYL, Reporters without Borders, Mission Lifeline and Luftbrücke Kabul\nare taking part in the programme as of 20 December 2022.774\n\nThe Government then takes the admission decision based on selection criteria that include vulnerability\n(in line with the UNHCR catalogue of criteria), relation to Germany e. g. through language skills, family\nties, previous stays or work for German authorities or projects, level of personal exposure of the person\ne. g. through a visible / exposed position or public statements, and a special political interest on the side\nof Germany to admit a person.775 As with the previous admission programme, selected persons first\nreceive assistance to leave Afghanistan and enter a neighbouring country and are then issued a visa and\ntravel assistance by the German embassy in that country. Persons who enter Germany under the\nprogramme receive a residence permit for three years. The Federal State responsible for reception of the\npersons is to be determined according to the quota system for the distribution of asylum seekers (see\nRegistration of the asylum application), although family ties and other ‘criteria supporting integration’ are\nto be taken into account.776\n\nWhen announcing the programme, the Federal Government declared that ‘the new programme is now to\nbe implemented quickly’ and that it planned to approve around 1,000 requests per month, which is about\nthe amount of permissions granted in the months preceding the announcement. The programme is\nplanned to run until the end of the current government’s term in 2025.777 As of 30 June 2023, 229 persons\nhave been selected for admission.778 As of October 2023, only 13 of them had been admitted, due to the\npause of the admission procedure (see below).779 According to a press report, the NGOs Mission Lifeline\nand Luftbrücke Kabul alone have received around 32,000 requests as of early November 2022.780\n\nIn March 2023, the Federal Foreign office declared that all admission programs would be put to a halt for\nan indefinite time due to alleged abuse. A speaker of the\nForeigner’s Office declared that there were no evidence supporting a systemic misuse of the admission\nprograms.782 Only in one case a person who applied for admission has been identified as a possible\n‘threat’ (‘Gefährder’). Nevertheless, the Foreigners Office along with the Minister of Interior decided to\nintroduce additional screening mechanisms and to halt the admission program until the screening\nmechanism is in place.783 The screening procedure involves an automatic data reconciliation with\ndatabases from the national security agencies and interviews with the German domestic intelligence\nservice (Bundesamt für Verfassungsschutz), Federal Criminal Police Office (Bundeskriminalamt), the\nFederal Police or BAMF officers acting on behalf of these national security services.784 The admission\nprograms were restarted from 26 June 2023. In the first months of the newly introduced screening\nprocedure (26 June – 21 July 2023) 99 screening interviews took place, no security concerns have been\nidentified among those.785 The Federal government further states, that the capacities for the screening\nmechanism have been expanded since its start. Accordingly, as of August 2023 several hundreds of\nscreenings can be conducted per month.786\n\nThe Left party and NGOs such as PRO ASYL welcomed the launch of the programme but criticised that\n1,000 admissions per month was too low given the ‘real pressure of persecution’ for ‘people who have\nfought for democracy and human rights’. PRO ASYL further criticised that the relatively abstract selection\ncriteria could lead to an ‘ethically highly ambivalent protection lottery’787 especially in connection with the\nfact that only authorised agencies could put forward people and that the application is through an\nalgorithm-based IT application with little room to put forward specific individual circumstances.788 The\nNGO Kabul Luftbrücke reported problems with the IT application in October and November, leading to\ndelays in sending the online forms to authorities. A further point of critique is that the programme does not\nextend to persons who have managed to flee Afghanistan.789 Several NGOs also voiced concerns over\nthe practical implementation, demanding a better staffing of the counselling and coordination centres for\nthe programme and questioning the ‘organization and content’ of the procedure,790 especially given that\nit is required to have a passport in order to leave the country while obtaining one is made extremely\ndifficult by the Taliban government. One year after the official launch, in October 2023, several NGOs\nreflected on the development of the program. They demanded that the originally envisaged number of\n1,000 admissions per months should be fulfilled, that the procedure should be more transparent and that\nin additional admission schemes are necessary to meet the needs for protection.791\n\nIn addition to the Federal Government, several Federal States (Thuringia, Berlin, Hessen and Bremen)\nhave implemented admission programmes based on family ties to Afghans living in the respective Federal\nStates (for more information see Family Reunification). At the same time, it has been reported that pro-Palestinian demonstrations\nhave been prohibited per se without any distinction to the cause they were protesting for, which amounted\nto a violation of the equal freedom of assembly. Additionally, the police have been criticised for its brutal\nreactions against pro-Palestinian demonstrations being in parts racially motivated.832 Some associations\nlike Jews and Palestinians for Peace and Combatants for Peace try to lead the public debate back to the\nfacts and a constructive exchange in providing information and workshops for schools and other\nassociations.833\n\nWhat impact the situation in the Palestinian territories and the political climate here in Germany have on\nPalestinian refugees in Germany is currently difficult to evaluate. The number of 743 asylum applicants\nin 2023 from Palestinian territories has significantly increased compared to 35 in 2022.834 This could be a\nresult of the escalating conflict. Another contributing factor to the increase of asylum applicants might\nhave been the court rulings of the Administrative Court in Oldenburg and of the Court of Justice of the\nEU. The Administrative Court in Oldenburg decided already in June 2023, prior to the escalation, that the\ncurrent situation in the West Bank amounts to a danger to the health and life of those living there and that\ntherefore persons present in Germany are eligible for toleration (‘Duldung’) under national law.835 The\nEuropean Court of Human Rights affirmed that UNRWA does no longer guarantee protection for\nPalestinians, making them eligible for national protection.836 Looking at the political debate, it seems that\nthere is a harsh climate not only with regard to the middle-east conflict but also vis-à-vis Palestinian\nrefugees. The Christian Democrats (CDU) affirmed that while humanitarian aid will be provided, migration\nflows to Germany should be prevented. The Social Democrats stated that the right to claim asylum applies\nto everyone equally and that possible security threats are checked for Palestinians as for every other\nasylum applicant.837\n\n\n\n\n832\n      Clara Neumann, Das Spannungsverhältnis zwischen Staatsräson und Grundrechten, 8 December 2023,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/3St683X.\n833\n      Tagesschau.de, Die Hoffnung auf Frieden, 16 December 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3w1d0ye;\n      WDR.de, Jüdin und Palästinenserin: “Ohne Dialog geht es nicht”, 19 November 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3unv70F.\n834\n      BAMF, Asylgeschäftsstatistik (statistics on applications, decisions and pending procedures), 1-12/2023,\n      available at: htps://bit.ly/3UjFWf0 1-12/2022 available at: https://bit.ly/3Z7UIUM.\n835\n      Administrative Court Oldenburg, Decision 3 A 3611/21, 7 June 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3waRhDM.\n836\n      CJEU, Decision C-294/22, 5 October 2023, available at: https://bit.ly/49noIBC.\n837\n      Rheinische Post.de, Union will keine palästinensischen Flüchtlinge, 18 October 2023, available in Germany\n      at: https://bit.ly/3up1I6n.\n\n\n                                                     129", "excerpt_sha256": "d8e907fef23b272391eac821c0af106821cbd666d7dcdd2480d8911d7936e535", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R04-E014", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany;
